📝 Practice Questions — Chapter 9

Q1

Why does hyperparameter tuning typically cost 10-100× more than a single training run?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Finding the best model requires running many experiments — each a full or partial training run. Grid search with 5 parameters × 4 values each = 1,024 trials. Even with 40 trials (Bayesian), that's 40× the cost of 1 training run.

💡 EXPLANATION
  COST MULTIPLIER BY SEARCH METHOD:
  
  Grid Search:    n_params ^ values_per_param
                  Example: 4 params, 4 values = 256 trials (256× single run)
  
  Random Search:  You choose N (typically 60-100)
                  Example: N=60 = 60× single run cost
  
  Bayesian Opt:   30-60 trials typical
                  Example: 40 trials = 40× single run cost
  
  Hyperband:      Evaluates 81, trains 40, completes 10
                  Example: ~15× single run cost (early stopping saves)
  
  🔑 RULE: Always budget experiment cost = single_run_cost × expected_trials.
  Never assume "one run and done."
Q2

What is the primary advantage of Bayesian optimization over Grid Search for hyperparameter tuning?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Bayesian optimization learns from previous trials to intelligently suggest the next parameters to try. It typically finds BETTER hyperparameters in 80% fewer trials than Grid Search, dramatically reducing experiment costs.

💡 EXPLANATION

Grid Search is "dumb" — it tries every combination regardless of results. If lr=0.1 gives terrible results, Grid Search still tries lr=0.1 with every other batch size. Bayesian optimization says "lr=0.1 is bad — let's not waste time there. Let's explore the region around lr=2e-5 which looks promising." It's the difference between searching for a needle in a haystack by examining every straw vs using a metal detector.

Q3

How does early stopping (Hyperband/ASHA) reduce hyperparameter tuning costs?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Early stopping kills unpromising trials early — after evaluating partial training progress. A trial that shows poor validation loss after 10% of training is unlikely to be the winner, so it's stopped, saving 90% of its cost.

💡 EXPLANATION
  HYPERBAND EXAMPLE (81 configurations, budget = 81 "epoch-units"):
  
  Round 1: Train all 81 configs for 1 epoch → keep top 27 (eliminate 54)
           Cost: 81 epoch-units
  
  Round 2: Train remaining 27 for 3 epochs → keep top 9 (eliminate 18)
           Cost: 81 epoch-units
  
  Round 3: Train remaining 9 for 9 epochs → keep top 1 (eliminate 8)
           Cost: 81 epoch-units
  
  TOTAL: 243 epoch-units to find best config
  GRID SEARCH (no early stop): 81 configs × 27 epochs = 2,187 epoch-units
  
  💰 HYPERBAND SAVES 89% vs Grid Search with no early stopping!
Q4

Why is experiment reproducibility important for FinOps?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Non-reproducible experiments force costly re-runs. If you can't reproduce a result, you must re-run the experiment to verify or compare — doubling the cost. Docker, fixed seeds, and config tracking prevent this.

💡 EXPLANATION

Without reproducibility: "The model accuracy dropped from 91% to 89%. Was it the new data? The new hyperparameters? Let me re-run the old experiment to compare." Cost: $258 re-run. With reproducibility: Every experiment has Docker image tag, git commit hash, exact config, and fixed seeds. You can reproduce ANY result exactly — no re-runs needed for comparison.

Q5

What metadata should you track for every experiment to enable FinOps cost analysis?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

GPU type, GPU hours, cost, hyperparameters, metric achieved, experiment ID, timestamp, and engineer. This enables cost-per-experiment analysis, cost-per-accuracy-gain metrics, and experiment budget tracking.

💡 EXPLANATION

Track in MLflow/W&B: experiment_id, hyperparameters, gpu_type, gpu_hours, cost_usd, validation_metric, status, engineer, docker_image_tag, git_commit. Build a dashboard: "Experiments this month: 45. Total cost: $1,800. Best accuracy: 91.2%. Cost per 1% accuracy gain: $150." This data drives optimization decisions.

Chapter 10: AI Data Cost Optimization 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 10: AI Data Cost Optimization — The Forgotten Multiplier

Data is the fuel of AI, but it's also a hidden cost center. Storage, transfer, preprocessing, and feature engineering can easily add 20-30% to your AI bill — and most teams don't track it. This chapter covers data pipeline optimization, efficient formats, feature stores, and lifecycle management with anihpj/jobpost examples.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 💾 Data 🎯 Core
🎓 Certification Relevance Level 1: Managing AI Data Sources Domain 3: AI Cost Optimization

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The full data pipeline cost map — where every dollar hides
  • ✅ Data format optimization: Parquet vs JSON vs CSV — 10× smaller, faster queries
  • ✅ Data lifecycle management: retention policies that save 80%+ on storage
  • ✅ Feature store economics: compute once, serve many — the cost-saving pattern
  • ✅ anihpj data pipeline: 500GB JSON → 50GB Parquet, 70% cost reduction

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — "Data Costs? I Thought GPUs Were the Problem"

GPUs are the obvious cost (89% of anihpj's bill). But data costs are the creeping cost. A 500GB dataset in JSON format costs $11.50/month in S3 Standard. That seems fine — until you have 10 copies (raw, cleaned, tokenized, train-split, val-split, etc.), versioned datasets, and model checkpoints. Suddenly it's $500/month in storage alone. Then add data transfer when you move data between regions. Then add the CPU/GPU cost of preprocessing. Data costs don't explode — they accumulate silently. FinOps catches them.

10.2 Efficient Data Storage for AI — Formats, Compression, Partitioning

FormatSize (500GB JSON eq.)Query SpeedCompressionAI Suitability
JSON500 GBSLOW — must parse entire fileNone (text)❌ Avoid for training data
CSV350 GBSlow — row-based scanningNone (text)❌ Avoid for large datasets
Parquet50 GBFAST — columnar, predicate pushdownSnappy/Gzip/Zstd✅ BEST for training data
ORC55 GBFast — columnar, optimized for HiveZlib/Snappy✅ Good (Hadoop ecosystem)
Avro120 GBMedium — row-based, schema evolutionSnappy/Deflate🟡 Streaming data only
TFRecord60 GBFast — TensorFlow nativeGzip✅ TensorFlow pipelines
📦
Why Parquet Wins for AI

(1) Columnar: Read only the columns you need (e.g., just 'title' and 'description' for BERT). JSON must parse everything. (2) Predicate pushdown: S3 Select/Athena can skip entire chunks. (3) Compression: 10× smaller than JSON. (4) Schema: Self-describing, no parsing errors. anihpj: 500GB JSON → 50GB Parquet. S3 cost: $11.50 → $1.15/month!

📅
Data Partitioning Strategy

Partition by date and source: s3://anihpj-data/jobpostings/year=2026/month=06/day=09/source=indeed/. Benefits: (1) Query only recent data — skip 95% of scans. (2) Lifecycle policies per partition (delete 2024 data, keep 2026). (3) Parallel processing per partition. Cost: Athena queries scan 5% of data instead of 100% — 20× cheaper!

10.3 Data Lifecycle Management — Auto-Archive and Auto-Delete

Data ages. Yesterday's raw logs are critical. Last year's raw logs are dead weight. Lifecycle policies automatically move data through storage tiers — saving up to 95% on storage costs.

Data Lifecycle — anihpj's Automated Tiering

  │  │ ─────────┼──────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────────────────││

  │  │          │              │          │                                      ││

  │  │ days     │ S3 Infreq    │          │ Older checkpoints (top 3 kept)       ││

  │  │          │ Archive      │          │                                      ││
  │  │          │              │          │                                      ││
  │  │ N/A      │ 🗑️ DELETE    │ $0.00    │ Failed experiment artifacts          ││
  │  │ (policy) │ (Expiration) │          │ Intermediate ETL temp files          ││
  │  │          │              │          │ Checkpoints beyond top-3             ││
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │                                                                              │
  │  💰 anihpj STORAGE COST — WITH LIFECYCLE POLICIES:                            │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ BEFORE: 50TB all in S3 Standard = $1,150/month                           ││
  │  │ AFTER:  2TB Hot + 10TB Warm + 20TB Cold + 18TB Frozen = $243/month      ││
  │  │ ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────             ││
  │  │ SAVINGS: $907/month (79% reduction!)                                     ││
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Data Catalog — Know What Data You Have (And What It Costs)

A data catalog answers: "What datasets do we have, who owns them, and what do they cost?" Without one, teams create duplicate datasets, store stale copies, and can't find existing data — so they recreate it (doubling costs).

📋
What a Data Catalog Tracks

Dataset name & description: What is this? Owner: Who created it? Location: S3 path, database table. Size & cost: Current GB and monthly $ impact. Last accessed: Is anyone using this? If not accessed in 90 days → archive candidate. Schema: Columns, types, meaning. Lineage: How was this dataset created? anihpj: AWS Glue Data Catalog + custom cost metadata tags.

💰
Cost Savings from Data Catalogs

Duplicate elimination: "Oh, Alice already has a cleaned job postings dataset in Parquet — I don't need to create my own." Saves $200/month in duplicate processing. Stale data cleanup: "This dataset hasn't been accessed in 6 months — archive to Glacier." Saves $45/month. Total savings: $245+/month for a team of 5 ML engineers. Tools: AWS Glue, Amundsen (OSS), DataHub (OSS), Atlan.

10.4 Feature Store Economics — Compute Once, Serve Many

A feature store is the cost-saving pattern of the AI world. Instead of recomputing features for every experiment, you compute once and reuse across all models and teams.

❌ WITHOUT Feature Store
  • Alice computes job embeddings for BERT training: $50
  • Bob computes SAME embeddings for LLaMA: $50
  • Carol computes SAME embeddings for XGBoost: $50
  • Total: $150 for identical computation!
  • Plus: Inconsistent features (Alice's version ≠ Bob's version)
✅ WITH Feature Store (Feast)
  • Compute job embeddings ONCE: $50
  • Store in Feast feature store (S3/DynamoDB)
  • Alice, Bob, Carol ALL read from feature store: $0 (already computed)
  • Total: $50 (67% savings)
  • Plus: Consistent features, versioned, documented
🏪
Popular Feature Stores

Feast (OSS, self-hosted): Free, runs on Kubernetes. Tecton (managed): $$, enterprise features. SageMaker Feature Store: AWS-native, pay-per-use. Vertex AI Feature Store: GCP-native. anihpj: Feast on EKS — $0/month for the store itself, pay only for underlying S3/DynamoDB.

💰
Feature Store ROI Calculation

Cost to compute: $50/run. Runs/month without store: 15 (3 teams × 5 experiments). Monthly cost without: $750. Monthly cost with: $50 (1 computation). Savings: $700/month. Feature store infrastructure: $100/month. Net savings: $600/month. Payback: 1 week.

Data Versioning — Track Data Like Code

Just as you version code with Git, you should version datasets. Without versioning, teams create duplicate copies of data "just in case" — exploding storage costs. Data versioning tools track changes without duplicating data.

📁
DVC (Data Version Control)

Git-like versioning for datasets and models. Stores metadata in Git, actual data in S3/GCS. Key feature: Deduplication — only stores changed files, not full copies. anihpj: 10 dataset versions × 500GB JSON each = 5TB without DVC. With DVC: ~550GB (only deltas stored). Storage savings: 89%. Free (OSS).

🌊
LakeFS (Data Lake Versioning)

Git-like branching for data lakes. Create branches for experiments, merge when confident. Key feature: Zero-copy branching — no data duplication. When to use: Multiple teams experimenting on same dataset, need rollback capability. Cost: Open-source or managed ($).

📝 Practice Questions — Chapter 10

Q1

Why is Parquet the recommended format for AI training data instead of JSON?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Parquet is columnar, compressed, and supports predicate pushdown. It's typically 10× smaller than JSON, queries scan only needed columns (20× faster), and compression (Snappy/Zstd) reduces storage and transfer costs dramatically.

💡 EXPLANATION
  JSON (500GB, text):  "title": "Software Engineer", "description": "We are..."
  Parquet (50GB, binary): Column 'title' stored separately from 'description'
  
  QUERY "SELECT title WHERE year=2026" on:
  JSON:     Must parse ALL 500GB to find matches
  Parquet:  Reads only 'title' and 'year' columns (~15GB), skips 'description'
            Predicate pushdown skips chunks where year≠2026
  
  RESULT: Parquet query costs 95% less in Athena (scanned bytes billed).
  Storage costs 90% less in S3.
Q2

What is a data lifecycle policy and how does it save money?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A lifecycle policy automatically transitions data between storage tiers (Hot→Warm→Cold→Delete) based on age. It saves 79-96% on storage costs because older, rarely-accessed data moves to progressively cheaper tiers.

💡 EXPLANATION

Example S3 Lifecycle Policy: Day 0-30: S3 Standard ($0.023/GB). Day 31-90: Transition to S3 Infrequent Access ($0.0125/GB — 46% savings). Day 91-365: Transition to S3 Glacier ($0.004/GB — 83% savings). Day 366+: Transition to Glacier Deep Archive ($0.001/GB — 96% savings). Day 730+: Delete. This is automatic — set once, runs forever.

Q3

How does a feature store reduce AI data costs?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

A feature store lets you compute features once and reuse them across all models and teams. Instead of 3 teams recomputing the same embeddings ($150), you compute once ($50) and everyone reads from the store. This saves 67%+ on repeated computation.

💡 EXPLANATION

Feature stores (Feast, Tecton, SageMaker Feature Store) act as a centralized repository for ML features. They provide: (1) Point-in-time correctness for training, (2) Low-latency serving for inference, (3) Feature reuse across models, (4) Versioning and lineage tracking. The cost savings come from eliminating redundant computation — the single biggest data cost in ML teams.

Q4

What is data partitioning and how does it reduce query costs?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Partitioning splits data by a column (e.g., date) into separate directories. Queries that filter on that column only scan relevant partitions — reducing scanned data by 90-95% and Athena/query costs proportionally.

💡 EXPLANATION
  WITHOUT PARTITIONING:
  s3://data/jobpostings/*.parquet (500GB, all files)
  Query "WHERE date='2026-06-09'": Scans ALL 500GB → $2.50 per query
  
  WITH PARTITIONING:
  s3://data/jobpostings/year=2026/month=06/day=09/*.parquet
  Query "WHERE date='2026-06-09'": Scans only day=09 partition (1.6GB) → $0.008
  
  💰 QUERY COST REDUCTION: 99.7%!
Q5

anihpj's data pipeline costs $600/month (500GB JSON, CPU preprocessing, no lifecycle). What three changes would you recommend first?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1. Convert JSON to Parquet (500GB→50GB, $11.50→$1.15/month storage). 2. Implement S3 lifecycle policies (Hot→Warm→Cold, 79% storage savings). 3. Switch preprocessing to GPU (RAPIDS cuDF on spot) ($24 CPU → $0.75 GPU, 97% savings on compute).

💡 EXPLANATION

These 3 changes — format conversion, lifecycle policies, GPU preprocessing — are the "low-hanging fruit" of data cost optimization. They require minimal engineering effort (1-2 weeks) and deliver 70%+ cost reduction: $600→$180/month. The order matters: Parquet first (enables everything else), lifecycle second (automatic savings), GPU preprocessing third (compute savings).

🚀 Part 4: AI Inference Cost Optimization Chapters 11-15

Chapter 11: Understanding Inference Costs 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 11: Understanding Inference Costs — The 70-90% of Your AI Bill

Training gets all the attention, but inference is where the real money goes. While training costs are fixed (you train once), inference costs grow with every user, every request, every second. Over a model's lifetime, inference typically accounts for 70-90% of total AI spend. This chapter teaches you how inference costs work, how to measure them, and why they're fundamentally different from training costs — using anihpj/jobpost as the running example.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🚀 Inference 🎯 Core
🎓 Certification Relevance Domain 3: AI Cost Optimization ~25% Level 3: AI Workload Optimization

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why inference dominates AI costs over time — the 70-90% rule
  • ✅ The 5 factors that drive inference costs and how they interact
  • ✅ How to measure inference cost: $/1K predictions, $/user, latency tradeoffs
  • ✅ anihpj's real inference cost baseline: $8,200/month serving 500 req/sec

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — "Training vs Inference — What's the Difference?"

Think of AI like a restaurant. Training is writing the recipe — you do it once (or periodically), it's expensive, and it takes time. Inference is cooking each meal for each customer — you do it millions of times, and while each meal is cheap ($0.00038 for anihpj), the total dwarfs the recipe cost. A restaurant that serves 1,000 meals/day spends far more on ingredients (inference) than on recipe development (training). Same with AI: training costs $2,100 once. But serving 500 requests/second costs $8,200 every single month. Over a year, inference costs 47× more than training.

11.1 Inference vs Training — Different Cost Profiles, Different Strategies

Training vs Inference — The Fundamental Differences
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    TRAINING vs INFERENCE — Two Different Worlds                  │
  │                                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │  🏋️ TRAINING                     │  🚀 INFERENCE                        ││

  │  │  • Fixed, one-time (or weekly)   │  • Continuous, grows with users      ││

  │  │  • Predictable: you choose when  │  • Unpredictable: users choose when  ││
  │  │                                  │                                      ││
  │  │  UTILIZATION PATTERN             │  UTILIZATION PATTERN                 ││
  │  │  • High during training (90%+)   │  • Variable: 15% night, 80% peak     ││
  │  │  • 0% between runs (idle)        │  • Must be always available          ││
  │  │  • Burst: 4-8 hours, then idle   │  • 24/7 service                      ││
  │  │                                  │                                      ││

  │  │  • Spot instances (60-90% off)   │  • Reserved instances (30-60% off)   ││
  │  │  • Checkpointing for safety      │  • Autoscaling (scale with demand)    ││
  │  │  • Mixed precision (FP16)        │  • Model quantization (INT8)          ││
  │  │  • Batch size tuning             │  • Dynamic batching                   ││
  │  │                                  │  • Caching                           ││
  │  │                                  │  • Model distillation                 ││
  │  │                                  │                                      ││
  │  │  RISK PROFILE                     │  RISK PROFILE                         ││
  │  │  • Can be interrupted (spot)     │  • Must be reliable (SLA: 99.9%)     ││
  │  │  • Retry is acceptable           │  • Interruption = user frustration    ││
  │  │  • Lost work = few minutes       │  • Lost request = lost revenue       ││
  │  └──────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The Cost Crossover — When Inference Overtakes Training

🔑 After 12 months, inference costs 12× more than training. Optimize inference FIRST.

11.2 Factors Affecting Inference Cost — The 5 Levers

🧠
1. Model Size (Parameters)

Impact: Linear — 2× bigger model = 2× more compute per request. BERT-base (110M): 0.44GB GPU memory. BERT-large (340M): 1.36GB. LLaMA-7B: 14GB. GPT-3 (175B): 350GB (needs 5×A100 just to load!). Strategy: Use smallest model that meets accuracy requirements. DistilBERT (66M) is 2× smaller than BERT with 95% accuracy.

📈
2. Request Volume (QPS)

Impact: Linear — 2× more requests = 2× more compute (if no batching). anihpj: 500 req/sec peak, 50 req/sec average. Without autoscaling, you pay for peak 24/7. With autoscaling, costs drop 60%+ during off-peak. Strategy: Autoscale based on request queue depth, not CPU.

📝
3. Input/Output Length

Impact: For LLMs, quadratic — 2× longer context = 4× compute (attention is O(n²)). For BERT, input is fixed (512 tokens). anihpj: Job descriptions average 200 tokens → BERT inference is fast. But if using LLM for job summaries, 2,000 token prompts = 10× more cost. Strategy: Truncate/pad to minimum needed length.

🎮
4. GPU Choice

Impact: H100 ($98/hr) vs T4 ($0.53/hr) = 185× difference. But H100 handles 10× more requests. Right-sizing rule: Match GPU throughput to traffic. Over-provisioned H100 at 15% util = 85% wasted money. T4 at 70% util may be cheaper per request. Strategy: Profile throughput, not just hourly rate.

📦
5. Batching Efficiency

Impact: 3-5× throughput improvement with zero hardware change. Batching 32 requests together on GPU uses the same memory as 1 request, but processes them simultaneously. Without batching: 120 req/sec on A10G. With dynamic batching: 470 req/sec on same A10G — 3.9× more throughput per dollar! Strategy: Always use dynamic batching (Triton, TorchServe, vLLM).

🔑 The Inference Cost Multiplier Formula

Monthly Inference Cost = (Requests/sec × Seconds/month × GPU Cost/sec) ÷ Batching_Efficiency ÷ GPU_Utilization. Example: 500 req/sec × 2.6M sec/month × ($5.67/3600 per sec) ÷ 3.5 (batching) ÷ 0.65 (util) = $8,200/month. Changing batching from 1→3.5 cuts cost by 71%. Raising GPU util from 25%→65% cuts cost by 62%. These two free changes reduce inference costs 89% — before touching hardware.

11.3 Measuring Inference Cost — The Metrics That Matter

MetricFormulaTargetWhy It Matters
Cost per 1K PredictionsTotal_Inference_Cost ÷ (Predictions ÷ 1000)<$0.50/1KStandard unit for comparing model serving costs
Cost per User/MonthTotal_Inference_Cost ÷ MAU<$1/userBusiness-friendly metric for execs
Inference Latency P9595th percentile response time<100msUser experience vs cost tradeoff
GPU UtilizationActual_TFLOPS ÷ Theoretical × 100>60%Higher = better cost efficiency
Requests per GPU-SecondThroughput ÷ GPU countMaximizeHardware efficiency metric

📝 Practice Questions — Chapter 11

Q1

Why does inference typically account for 70-90% of total AI costs over a model's lifetime?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Training is a fixed one-time cost whereas inference is continuous and scales with users. A $2,100 training run is paid once. $8,200/month inference for 12 months = $98,400 — 47× more than training.

💡 EXPLANATION
  COST OVER 12 MONTHS:
  Training: $2,100 × 1 (or 12 if weekly retraining) = $2,100-$25,200
  Inference: $8,200/month × 12 = $98,400
  
  TRAINING IS LIKE BUYING A CAR (one-time capital expense).
  INFERENCE IS LIKE BUYING GAS (continuous operational expense).
  Over the car's lifetime, gas costs far exceed the car's purchase price.
Q2

Name the five factors that drive inference costs.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Model size, request volume, input/output length, GPU choice, and batching efficiency. Each factor linearly or quadratically multiplies the base compute cost.

💡 EXPLANATION

Model size: 2× bigger = 2× cost. Volume: 2× requests = 2× cost. I/O length: 2× tokens = 2-4× cost (quadratic for attention). GPU: H100 costs 185× more than T4 but processes 10× more. Batching: 3-5× throughput gain for free.

Q3

What is the standard unit for measuring inference cost, and what's a good target?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Cost per 1,000 predictions. A good target is <$0.50/1K predictions for most models. anihpj's current baseline is $0.38/1K, targeting $0.08/1K after optimization.

💡 EXPLANATION

Cost per 1K predictions normalizes across different traffic volumes, making it the best metric for comparing serving efficiency between models, teams, and time periods. It's to inference what "cost per training run" is to training.

Q4

anihpj runs 2 GPUs 24/7 at $11.34/hr. Night traffic is 20 req/sec vs 500 during peak. What's the most impactful first optimization?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Autoscaling — scale down to 1 GPU (or 0) during night hours. This alone saves ~40% of inference cost ($3,312/month) with zero code changes to the model.

💡 EXPLANATION
  WITHOUT AUTOSCALING: 2 GPUs × 24hrs × 30 days × $5.67 = $8,165/month
  WITH AUTOSCALING:
    Peak (8hrs):   2 GPUs × 8hrs × 30 × $5.67 = $2,722
    Off-Peak (6hrs): 1 GPU × 6hrs × 30 × $5.67 = $1,021
    Night (10hrs): 0 GPUs × 10hrs × 30 × $0 = $0 (scale-to-zero!)
    ────────────────────────────────────────────
    TOTAL: $3,743/month
  
  SAVINGS: $4,422/month (54% reduction!)
Q5

What's the key difference in optimization strategy between training and inference?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Training optimization focuses on spot instances and throughput (fault-tolerant, burst). Inference optimization focuses on autoscaling, batching, and model compression (always-on, latency-sensitive, scales with users).

💡 EXPLANATION

Training: Can be interrupted → use spot instances (60-90% off). Can batch arbitrarily → maximize GPU utilization. Inference: Cannot be interrupted → reserved instances (30-60% off). Must meet latency SLAs → autoscale, not just maximize throughput. Different problems, different solutions.

Chapter 12: Model Optimization for Cost-Efficient Inference 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 12: Model Optimization for Cost-Efficient Inference — Make Models Smaller, Faster, Cheaper

You don't always need a bigger GPU. Often, the best way to reduce inference costs is to make the model itself more efficient. Quantization, distillation, pruning, and architecture choices can reduce inference costs by 50-90% — with minimal accuracy loss. This chapter covers all five techniques with real anihpj examples.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~2 hours 🔧 Optimization 🎯 Core
🎓 Certification Relevance Domain 3: AI Cost Optimization ~25%

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — "Why Make the Model Smaller?"

Imagine you deliver pizzas. A full-size truck (FP32 model) can carry 100 pizzas but uses lots of gas. A compact car (INT8 model) carries 95 pizzas but uses 75% less gas. Same deliveries, drastically lower cost. Quantization, distillation, and pruning are ways to convert your "truck" model into a "compact car" model — sacrificing a tiny bit of capacity for massive cost savings. For most business applications (like anihpj job matching), the 1-2% accuracy difference is imperceptible to users but saves thousands per month.

12.1 Model Quantization — The #1 Inference Cost Optimizer

Quantization reduces numerical precision: FP32 → FP16 → INT8 → INT4. Each step roughly doubles speed and halves memory with minimal accuracy loss.

PrecisionBits/WeightModel Size (BERT)Speed vs FP32Accuracy DropCost Reduction
FP3232440 MB1× (baseline)——
FP1616220 MB2×<0.1%50%
INT88110 MB4×<1%75%
INT4455 MB6-8×1-5%87%
🔧
Post-Training Quantization (PTQ)

Quantize an already-trained model. PyTorch: model = torch.quantization.quantize_dynamic(model, dtype=torch.qint8). ONNX Runtime: Auto-quantizes during export. TensorRT: One-command INT8 calibration. Accuracy: Typically <1% loss. anihpj: BERT PTQ to INT8: $8,200 → $2,050/month with 0.3% accuracy drop. Always try PTQ first.

🎯
Quantization-Aware Training (QAT)

Train WITH quantization simulation. Model learns to compensate for precision loss. When: PTQ accuracy loss is unacceptable (rare for inference). Cost: One extra training run ($258 for anihpj). Result: INT8 with <0.1% accuracy loss. Trade-off: Higher upfront cost, better long-term inference savings.

12.2 Model Distillation — Train a Smaller Student from a Large Teacher

Distillation trains a small "student" model to mimic a large "teacher" model. The student can be 10-50× smaller while retaining 95%+ of the teacher's accuracy.

👨‍🏫 TEACHER (Large)
  • BERT-base: 110M params, 440MB
  • Inference: $8,200/month (2×A10G)
  • Accuracy: 91.2%
🎓 STUDENT (Small)
  • DistilBERT: 66M params, 265MB (40% smaller)
  • Inference: $3,280/month (1×A10G) — 60% savings!
  • Accuracy: 90.5% (0.7% drop)
  • One-time distillation cost: $412 (spot training)

💰 Payback period: 2 days. Annual savings: $59,040!

💡 When to Distill

✅ Distill when: High inference volume (>100 req/sec), model is large (>100M params), latency SLA is tight. ❌ Skip when: Low volume (distillation cost > savings), model is already small, accuracy loss unacceptable (medical/safety-critical). Popular student models: DistilBERT (BERT→66M), TinyBERT (BERT→14M), MobileBERT (BERT→25M), Llama-3B (distilled from Llama-70B).

12.3 Model Pruning — Remove Unnecessary Weights

Pruning removes weights/neurons that contribute little to the output. A typical neural network has 50-90% redundant weights that can be removed with minimal accuracy impact.

✂️
Magnitude Pruning (Simple)

Remove weights with smallest absolute values. "If a weight is close to zero, it probably doesn't matter." Result: 50-70% sparsity, 2-3× speedup on hardware that supports sparsity (A100/H100). PyTorch: torch.nn.utils.prune.l1_unstructured(module, 'weight', 0.5)

🏗️
Structured Pruning (Hardware-Friendly)

Remove entire neurons/channels/attention heads — not individual weights. Result: Actually smaller and faster on ALL hardware (not just sparse-capable). 30-50% size reduction, 1.5-2× speedup. Trade-off: More accuracy loss than unstructured, but real speed gains on any GPU.

🔑 Pruning Reality Check

Pruning sounds amazing but has caveats: (1) Unstructured pruning (individual weights) only speeds up on A100/H100 with hardware sparsity support. (2) Often requires retraining after pruning to recover accuracy. (3) Quantization usually gives better cost savings with less effort. Recommendation: Try quantization first. If model is still too big/slow, then try pruning or distillation.

12.4 Model Architecture Choices — Design for Cost from Day One

ArchitectureParamsSpeed vs BERTAccuracyBest For
BERT-base110M1×91.2%Baseline — good accuracy, moderate cost
DistilBERT66M1.6× faster90.5%Best balance — 40% smaller, 95% accuracy
ALBERT12M*0.8× slower89.0%Memory-constrained (12M params but deeper)
TinyBERT14M4× faster87.5%Edge/mobile, extreme cost sensitivity
Flash AttentionSame2-4× faster0% lossDrop-in replacement — must use for LLMs!

* ALBERT shares parameters across layers — 12M unique params but 12-layer depth.

Advanced Architecture Patterns for Cost Reduction

🧩
Mixture of Experts (MoE)

How: Model has multiple "expert" sub-networks. Each input activates only a subset (e.g., 2 of 8 experts). Result: 8× more total parameters but only 2× compute cost per request. Example: Mixtral 8×7B — 47B total params but only 13B active per token. Inference cost = 13B model cost, capability = 47B model. Cost impact: 70% cheaper than equivalent dense model for same capability. Trade-off: Higher memory (all experts loaded), but compute-efficient.

🔮
Speculative Decoding

How: Small draft model predicts tokens → large model verifies in parallel. Result: 2-3× faster generation with zero quality loss. Example: Llama 3 8B draft + Llama 3 70B verification. Draft generates 5 tokens, 70B checks all 5 in one pass. Cost: Draft model adds ~10% compute overhead, but net 2-3× speedup. Best for: LLM generation tasks (chat, code, summaries).

💡 anihpj Architecture Decision

For job matching inference: DistilBERT + INT8 quantization. Result: 66M params → 110MB INT8 model. Throughput: 900 req/sec on 1×A10G. Cost: $4,140 → $1,035/month with INT8. Accuracy: 90.5% (vs 91.2% BERT). Business impact: 0.7% accuracy loss = negligible user impact. 75% cost savings = meaningful business impact. Architecture is a FinOps decision, not just an ML decision.

12.5 Hardware-Aware Model Optimization — TensorRT, ONNX, OpenVINO

🔧
NVIDIA TensorRT (Best for NVIDIA GPUs)

Compiles and optimizes models specifically for NVIDIA GPUs. Optimizations: Layer fusion, kernel auto-tuning, precision calibration (FP16/INT8), memory optimization. Result: 3-5× throughput improvement on same GPU. Cost: Free (included in NVIDIA NGC containers). anihpj: TensorRT-optimized BERT on A10G: 120→470 req/sec — 3.9× more throughput per GPU dollar!

🔀
ONNX Runtime (Cross-Platform)

Open standard for model exchange. Optimizes for CPU, GPU, and edge. Best for: Multi-platform deployment (train on NVIDIA, serve on Intel CPU). Result: 2-3× speedup on CPU, 1.5-2× on GPU. Trade-off: Less optimized than TensorRT for NVIDIA specifically, but portable.

🔵
OpenVINO (Intel Hardware)

Intel's inference optimizer for CPUs, VPUs, and integrated GPUs. Best for: CPU-only inference (no GPU available), edge devices. Result: 3-5× speedup on Intel Xeon CPUs. anihpj use case: If serving job matching on CPU-only instances ($0.50/hr vs $5.67/hr for GPU) — OpenVINO makes CPU inference viable for low-traffic scenarios.

📊
Which Optimizer When?

NVIDIA GPU → TensorRT (best performance). Multi-platform → ONNX Runtime (portability). Intel CPU → OpenVINO (CPU optimization). Apple Silicon → Core ML. Rule: Always use an inference optimizer. Raw PyTorch/TensorFlow serving is 2-5× slower than optimized serving.

📝 Practice Questions — Chapter 12

Q1

What is model quantization and why is it the #1 inference cost optimization?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Reducing numerical precision (FP32→INT8) for 4× faster inference, 75% less memory, and 75% cost reduction — with <1% accuracy loss. It's the highest-impact, lowest-effort optimization for inference.

💡 EXPLANATION
  QUANTIZATION IMPACT (BERT-base):
  FP32: 440MB model, 120 req/sec, $8,200/month
  INT8: 110MB model, 470 req/sec, $2,050/month
  
  SAME GPU, SAME MODEL, 75% LESS COST.
  Implementation: 1 line of code in PyTorch/TensorRT/ONNX.
  
  EXAM TIP: If a question asks "what's the FIRST optimization
  for inference?" → Quantization to INT8.
Q2

What's the difference between Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) and Quantization-Aware Training (QAT)?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

PTQ quantizes an already-trained model (fast, ~1% accuracy loss). QAT trains with quantization simulation (requires retraining, <0.1% accuracy loss). Always try PTQ first — it's sufficient for 95%+ of use cases.

Q3

How does model distillation reduce inference costs?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Trains a smaller "student" model to mimic a larger "teacher." The student is 40-95% smaller but retains 95%+ accuracy. anihpj: BERT→DistilBERT reduced inference from $8,200 to $3,280/month with one-time $412 distillation cost.

Q4

What does NVIDIA TensorRT do and how much does it improve inference throughput?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

TensorRT compiles and optimizes models for NVIDIA GPUs — layer fusion, kernel tuning, precision calibration. Typical improvement: 3-5× throughput on the SAME GPU. Free with NVIDIA containers. anihpj: 120→470 req/sec on A10G.

Q5

Rank these optimization techniques by typical cost savings: pruning, quantization, distillation, architecture change.

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

1. Quantization (75%), 2. Distillation (60%), 3. Architecture (40-60%), 4. Pruning (30-50%). Quantization wins because it's free, fast, and works on any model. Distillation requires one training run. Architecture changes require model redesign.

Chapter 13: Inference Infrastructure Optimization 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 13: Inference Infrastructure Optimization — Right-Size, Autoscale, Cache, Serve

You've optimized your model. Now optimize the infrastructure that serves it. Right-sizing GPUs, autoscaling, dynamic batching, multi-model serving, and caching can reduce inference costs by 40-80% — beyond what model optimization alone can achieve. This chapter covers all six infrastructure optimization techniques with anihpj implementations.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🏗️ Infrastructure🎯 Core
🎓 Certification Relevance Domain 3: AI Cost Optimization Level 3: Architecting & Workload Placement

🧑‍🏫 Novice's Corner — "Infrastructure Optimization vs Model Optimization"

Model optimization (Ch 12) makes the model itself faster. Infrastructure optimization (Ch 13) makes the serving system smarter — scaling up/down with traffic, batching requests efficiently, and sharing GPU resources across models. Think of it like a restaurant: model optimization is making each dish faster to cook. Infrastructure optimization is adding more cooks during rush hour and sharing ovens efficiently. You need BOTH for maximum savings.

13.1 Right-Sizing Inference Hardware — Match GPU to Throughput

GPU$/hrMax Req/s (BERT)$/1M RequestsBest Traffic Level
T4$0.5380$1.84<50 req/s (dev/light)
L4$0.80140$1.5950-150 req/s
A10G$5.67470$3.35150-500 req/s
L40S$121,100$3.03500-3000 req/s
H100$984,500$6.05>3000 req/s (LLMs only)

anihpj: 500 req/sec peak. A10G handles 470 req/sec. 2×A10G at $11.34/hr = $8,278/month. Could 1×L40S at $12/hr serve 500 req/s? Yes — and save 25% ($8,760 vs $8,278... wait, L40S is MORE expensive per hour!). Lesson: Always calculate $/request, not just $/hour.

CPU Inference — When It's Actually Cheaper Than GPU

🖥️
CPU Inference Economics

When CPU wins: Very low traffic (<5 req/sec), tiny models (<10M params), or batch processing with no latency SLA. Example: DistilBERT INT8 on c5.xlarge (4 vCPU, $0.17/hr): 15 req/sec. A10G GPU ($5.67/hr): 470 req/sec. Cost/request: CPU = $0.0031, GPU = $0.00335 — basically equal! But CPU needs 31× more instances for same throughput. Rule: CPU cheaper per-request at very low volumes. GPU essential for any production scale.

🔧
CPU Optimization with OpenVINO

Intel OpenVINO optimizes models for CPU inference. Result: 3-5× speedup on Intel Xeon. DistilBERT INT8 + OpenVINO on c5.xlarge: 45 req/sec (3× baseline). Use case: anihpj dev/staging inference on CPU instances ($0.17/hr vs $5.67/hr GPU) — saves $5.50/hr for non-production traffic. Production: Still GPU for latency SLA. Dev/Staging: CPU + OpenVINO for cost savings.

13.2 Dynamic Batching — 3-5× Throughput for Free

GPUs excel at parallel computation. Sending 1 request at a time wastes 80%+ of GPU capacity. Dynamic batching groups requests together automatically.

📦
How Dynamic Batching Works

Triton Server waits a configurable time window (e.g., 100μs), collects all incoming requests, and processes them as one batch on the GPU. Result: 120 req/sec (no batching) → 470 req/sec (batching). Same GPU, 3.9× more throughput. Cost: Free — just a Triton config change. Trade-off: Adds 100μs-5ms latency. For most applications, imperceptible.

⚙️
Batching Configuration Tips

Max batch size: Set to GPU memory limit (test with largest inputs). Delay: 100μs-1ms typical. Too high = latency complaints. Too low = poor batching. Queue policy: Use Triton's dynamic batcher (not static). anihpj: max_batch_size=32, max_queue_delay_microseconds=500. Result: 3.9× throughput at P95 latency of 12ms (well under 50ms SLA).

13.3 Autoscaling for Inference — Pay for What You Use

Autoscaling anihpj Inference — From Flat Cost to Traffic-Proportional
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    AUTOSCALING IMPACT — anihpj Inference                         │
  │                                                                              │

  │  2 GPUs × $5.67/hr × 730hrs = $8,278/month                                   │
  │                                                                              │
  │  WITH KEDA AUTOSCALING (scale on request queue depth):                        │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ PERIOD          │ TRAFFIC  │ GPUs │ HRS/DAY │ COST/DAY   │ COST/MONTH  ││
  │  │ ────────────────┼──────────┼──────┼─────────┼────────────┼─────────────││
  │  │ Peak (9AM-5PM)  │ 500 r/s  │  2   │   8     │ $90.72     │ $2,722      ││

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔑 Scale-to-Zero is the Holy Grail

With KEDA or Knative, inference pods scale to ZERO when there's no traffic. Cold start (1-2 seconds to load model) is acceptable for non-real-time services. anihpj saves 10 hours/day × $11.34/hr = $3,402/month just by scaling to zero overnight. This alone pays for the entire FinOps team.

13.4 Multi-Model Serving — One GPU, Many Models

🧩
NVIDIA MIG (Multi-Instance GPU)

Physically partitions one A100/H100 into up to 7 isolated GPU instances. Each runs a different model. A100 80GB: 7× 1g.10gb (10GB each) or 3× 2g.20gb or 1× 7g.80gb (full). Cost benefit: Instead of 3×T4 for 3 models ($1.59/hr), 1×A100 with MIG serves all 3 ($33/hr). Better if models need >16GB VRAM. anihpj: JobBERT + SkillBERT + ResumeBERT all on 1×A100 via MIG.

⏱️
GPU Time-Slicing (Software)

Triton can run multiple models on one GPU without MIG — just time-slicing. Simpler than MIG but less isolation. Best for: Models that together fit in GPU memory. Cost benefit: 3 small models on 1×A10G ($5.67/hr) vs 3×T4 ($1.59/hr). A10G gives better performance per dollar for mixed workloads.

13.5 Caching Strategies — Don't Compute the Same Answer Twice

💾
Result Caching (Redis)

Cache frequent predictions. "Software Engineer in NY" → same top-20 jobs every time. Cache hit rate: 30-40% for job search (popular queries repeat). Result: 30-40% fewer GPU inference calls. Cost: Redis/ElastiCache ~$50/month. Savings: $2,484-$3,312/month (30-40% of $8,278). ROI: 50-66×.

📊
Embedding Cache (Vector DB)

Pre-compute and cache embeddings for static content (job postings). Only compute the user's query embedding in real-time. anihpj: 50M job embeddings pre-computed ($50 one-time). Each search: compute 1 user embedding + cosine similarity against cached embeddings. Savings: 98% fewer embedding computations.

13.6 Edge & Hybrid Inference — Push Compute to the User

For latency-critical or offline scenarios, run inference on-device. Edge inference = zero cloud GPU cost. Trade-off: smaller models, less accuracy.

✅ EDGE (On-Device)
  • TinyBERT (14MB) on mobile phone
  • Zero cloud GPU cost
  • Works offline
  • <10ms latency
  • Accuracy: 87.5% (vs 91.2% cloud BERT)
  • anihpj: Career fair kiosk with Jetson Nano ($99 one-time)
⚠️ CLOUD (Server)
  • BERT-base on A10G GPU
  • $8,278/month
  • Requires internet
  • 95ms latency
  • Accuracy: 91.2%
  • anihpj: Main web/mobile inference

📝 Practice Questions — Chapter 13

Q1

What's the most impactful infrastructure optimization for inference costs?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Autoscaling (including scale-to-zero). It matches GPU count to actual traffic, eliminating idle GPU costs during off-peak hours. anihpj saved 55% ($4,536/month) by scaling down at night.

💡 EXPLANATION

Autoscaling is the #1 infrastructure optimization because it requires NO model changes and NO accuracy tradeoffs — just infrastructure config. KEDA or Knative can scale based on request queue depth or GPU utilization. Scale-to-zero during zero-traffic periods is the holy grail.

Q2

How does dynamic batching improve inference cost efficiency?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Groups multiple requests into one GPU execution, using parallel compute capacity that would otherwise be wasted. Typical improvement: 3-5× throughput on the same GPU — 70-80% cost per request reduction. Free with Triton.

Q3

T4 costs $0.53/hr and handles 80 req/sec. A10G costs $5.67/hr and handles 470 req/sec. Which is more cost-effective per request?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

T4: $0.53 ÷ 80 × 1M = $1.84/1M requests. A10G: $5.67 ÷ 470 × 1M = $3.35/1M requests. T4 is 45% cheaper per request — despite being "slower." Always calculate $/request, not $/hour.

Q4

What's the difference between MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) and GPU time-slicing?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

MIG physically partitions GPU hardware (isolated memory, cache, compute). Time-slicing shares GPU in software (less isolation, simpler). MIG is better for strict multi-tenancy and security. Time-slicing is simpler for sharing across your own models.

Q5

When does edge inference (on-device) make financial sense vs cloud inference?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

When: (1) latency requirement <10ms (cloud adds 50-100ms round-trip), (2) offline capability needed, (3) privacy regulations prohibit cloud, (4) very high request volume makes cloud costs prohibitive. Trade-off: smaller model, ~3-5% lower accuracy.

Chapter 14: Inference Serving Platforms — Cost Comparison 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 14: Inference Serving Platforms — Choose the Right Tool for the Job

Where you serve your model matters as much as how you optimize it. Self-managed Triton on Kubernetes, managed SageMaker, serverless GPU functions, or API services — each has dramatically different cost profiles. This chapter compares all options with TCO analysis for anihpj's workload.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🏗️ Platforms🎯 Core
🎓 Certification Relevance Level 3: Licensing & SaaS Decisions

14.1 Self-Managed vs Managed Inference — The TCO Equation

FactorSelf-Managed (Triton/K8s)Managed (SageMaker)
GPU Cost$5.67/hr (A10G spot)$7.94/hr (40% markup)
Ops Cost0.25 FTE ($25K/yr)Included in markup
Setup Time2-4 weeks1-2 days
Annual TCO (2 GPUs)$124K (GPU + ops)$139K (GPU markup + endpoint fees)

🔑 The Break-Even Rule

Self-managed is cheaper at >$5K/month inference spend. Below that, managed services' markup is less than the cost of hiring ops expertise. anihpj at $8,278/month: self-managed saves ~$15K/year. At $2,000/month: managed is probably cheaper when you factor in engineering time.

14.2 Triton Inference Server — The Gold Standard

🚀
Why Triton Wins for FinOps

(1) Dynamic batching: 3-5× throughput for free. (2) Concurrent model execution: Run multiple models on one GPU. (3) Model ensembles: Chain models into pipelines (preprocess→BERT→postprocess) on GPU — no CPU handoff. (4) Performance analyzer: Built-in tool to find optimal batch size, instance count, and GPU config. (5) GPU metrics export to Prometheus. Cost: Free (OSS, NVIDIA maintained).

🔧
Alternatives to Triton

TorchServe: PyTorch-native, simpler but less optimized. BentoML: Great DX, built-in optimization. Ray Serve: Python-first, good for complex pipelines. Seldon Core: K8s-native, complex but powerful. vLLM: Best for LLMs specifically (PagedAttention, continuous batching). anihpj recommendation: Triton for general models, vLLM for LLMs.

14.3 Serverless GPU Inference — Pay Per Request

⚡
Serverless Options

Replicate: Pay per second of GPU time. Cold start: 30s-2min. Modal: Python-native serverless GPU. Banana: Simple API, per-request pricing. HuggingFace Inference Endpoints: Managed, per-hour + per-request. Best for: Burst traffic (<100 req/hr average, spikes to 1000), prototyping, side projects. Not for: Steady high-volume inference (cheaper to self-host).

💰
Serverless vs Dedicated Break-Even

Serverless (Replicate): $0.000725/sec GPU ($2.61/hr equivalent). Wait, that's CHEAPER than T4! But: cold starts, rate limits, no batching optimization. For anihpj at 500 req/sec 24/7: serverless would cost $45,000/month (per-request pricing adds up). Dedicated: $3,742/month with autoscaling. Serverless wins for <100 req/hr average.

14.4 Model Endpoints as a Service — API-First AI

14.5 anihpj Inference Platform Decision

🏆 anihpj Recommendation: Self-Managed Triton on EKS

Why: (1) $8,278/month spend justifies self-managed (break-even is $5K). (2) Triton dynamic batching = 3.9× throughput (470 req/sec on 1 A10G). (3) KEDA autoscaling = 55% savings ($3,742/month). (4) Full control over GPU type, region, and spot/on-demand mix. (5) Existing Kubernetes expertise (they already run EKS). Stack: EKS + Triton + KEDA + Prometheus + Grafana. Estimated cost after all optimizations: $1,800/month (vs $8,278 baseline). Savings: 78%.

Chapter 15: LLM-Specific Cost Optimization 🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 15: LLM-Specific Cost Optimization — When Billions of Parameters Meet Millions of Users

LLMs (GPT-4, Llama, Claude) introduce cost challenges that traditional ML models don't face: $0.03-0.12 per 1K tokens, quadratic attention costs, and massive GPU memory requirements. This chapter covers LLM-specific optimization techniques — from API cost management to self-hosting to RAG — that can mean the difference between a $2,000/month LLM bill and a $200,000/month one.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~2 hours🤖 LLMs🎯 Core
🎓 Certification Relevance Domain 3: AI Cost Optimization ~25% Level 3: AI Workload Optimization

15.1 Why LLMs Are a Cost Challenge — The Numbers Are Scary

ModelParamsGPU Memory NeededAPI $/1M TokensCost to Serve 1M Users
BERT-base110M0.44 GB (1×T4)N/A (self-host)$8,200/month
Llama 3 8B8B16 GB (1×A10G)$0.20/M$3,500/month (API)
Llama 3 70B70B140 GB (2×A100)$0.90/M$28,000/month (API)
GPT-4 (est.)~1.7T3,400 GB (43×A100!)$30/M input, $60/M output$500K+/month (API)

Context window impact: Attention is O(n²). 32K context = 1,024× more compute than 1K context. A 128K context GPT-4 call can cost $10+ for a single request! Prompt compression is not optional — it's financial survival.

15.2 LLM Inference Optimization Techniques

📄
KV-Cache Optimization (PagedAttention)

vLLM's key innovation: manages attention cache in fixed-size "pages" instead of contiguous memory. Result: 2-4× higher throughput, 50%+ less memory waste. Cost: Free (OSS, vLLM). Must-use for any LLM serving.

🔮
Speculative Decoding

Small draft model generates candidate tokens, large model verifies them. Result: 2-3× faster generation with zero quality loss. Cost: Needs a small draft model. Best for: LLMs generating long responses (summarization, code gen).

📦
Continuous Batching (vLLM/TGI)

Unlike static batching (wait for batch to fill), continuous batching adds/removes requests dynamically. Result: 10-20× higher throughput vs naive batching for LLMs. Why it matters: LLMs have variable output lengths — static batching leaves GPUs idle.

🔢
LLM Quantization (GPTQ/AWQ/GGUF)

GPTQ: 4-bit quantization for GPU inference (2-3× speed, 75% less memory). AWQ: Activation-aware, better accuracy than GPTQ. GGUF: CPU-focused quantization (llama.cpp). anihpj for Llama 8B: AWQ 4-bit = 5.5GB VRAM (fits on 1×T4 instead of A10G!).

⚡
Flash Attention (Must-Use for LLMs)

Problem: Standard attention is O(n²) — 32K context = 1,024× more compute than 1K. Solution: Flash Attention restructures computation to avoid materializing the full attention matrix in GPU memory. Result: 2-4× faster, 50-75% less memory, mathematically identical output. Availability: Built into PyTorch 2.0+, HuggingFace, vLLM. Cost: Free — just enable with torch.backends.cuda.enable_flash_sdp(). For LLM serving, Flash Attention is not optional — it's mandatory for cost efficiency.

15.3 LLM API Cost Management — OpenAI, Anthropic, Together

ProviderModel$ / 1M Input Tokens$ / 1M Output TokensBest For
OpenAIGPT-4o$5.00$15.00Most capable, most expensive
OpenAIGPT-4o-mini$0.15$0.60Best value — 33× cheaper than GPT-4o
AnthropicClaude 3.5 Sonnet$3.00$15.00Long context (200K), code
Together AILlama 3 70B$0.90$0.90Open-source, self-host alternative
Together AILlama 3 8B$0.20$0.20Cheapest capable LLM — 75× cheaper than GPT-4o
🔑 API Cost Rule: Model Cascade Saves 80%+

Don't use GPT-4o for everything. Cascade: (1) Try GPT-4o-mini first — if confident, return. (2) If unsure, escalate to GPT-4o. (3) For high-volume simple tasks (classification, extraction), use Llama 3 8B. Result: 80% of requests handled by mini models, 20% by large models. Blended cost: 75% cheaper than all-GPT-4o.

Prompt Compression & Token Counting — Pay for What You Need

📏
Token Counting — Know Before You Send

LLMs charge per token. Count tokens BEFORE sending: tiktoken (OpenAI), transformers tokenizer. 1 token ≈ 0.75 English words. "Software Engineer with 5 years Python experience" = 9 tokens. Cost awareness: 10K token prompt to GPT-4o = $0.05. 100K daily = $150/month. 128K context prompt (max) = $0.64 per request! Rule: Always truncate prompts to minimum needed length. Don't send entire documents when summaries suffice.

✂️
Prompt Compression Techniques

1. Summarize before sending: Use cheaper model (GPT-4o-mini) to summarize long docs, then send summary to expensive model (GPT-4o). 2. Remove stop words: "The candidate has experience in..." → "Candidate: 5yr Python, 3yr AWS." 3. Structured prompts: JSON/XML format uses fewer tokens than prose. 4. Cache system prompts: OpenAI caches repeated prefixes — keep system prompt identical. Savings: 50-70% prompt cost reduction with compression.

LLM Serving Frameworks — vLLM vs TGI vs TensorRT-LLM

FrameworkKey FeatureThroughput GainBest For
vLLMPagedAttention — manages KV-cache in pages2-4× vs naiveGeneral LLM serving. Best all-around. OSS.
TGI (HuggingFace)Continuous batching + watermarking2-3× vs naiveHuggingFace ecosystem. Good for HF models.
TensorRT-LLMNVIDIA-optimized kernels + quantization3-5× vs naiveMaximum NVIDIA GPU performance. More complex.
llama.cppCPU-focused, GGUF quantization1-2× (CPU)CPU inference, edge, local dev. Not for server.

anihpj recommendation: vLLM for Llama 3 8B serving. Best balance of performance, ease of use, and community support. If maximum throughput needed on H100, use TensorRT-LLM.

15.5 RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) — Cost-Saving Architecture

RAG replaces "put everything in the prompt" with "retrieve relevant docs, then generate." Cost insight: Embedding + retrieval is 100-1000× cheaper than stuffing context into the LLM prompt.

❌ WITHOUT RAG (Context Stuffing)
  • "Here are 50 job postings [25K tokens]. Rank them."
  • Cost (GPT-4o): 25K input tokens = $0.125 per request
  • At 100K requests/day: $12,500/day = $375,000/month 🚨
✅ WITH RAG
  • Embed query (1 token) → retrieve top-10 jobs → "Rank these 10 [5K tokens]"
  • Cost (GPT-4o): 0.1K embed + 5K input = $0.025 per request
  • At 100K requests/day: $2,500/day = $75,000/month
  • RAG saves 80%: $375K → $75K!

Vector Database Costs — The RAG Infrastructure

Vector DBPricingBest ForMonthly Cost (1M vectors)
pgvector (PostgreSQL)Free (OSS, runs on your RDS)Already using PostgreSQL$0 (existing DB)
Pinecone$0.058/GB + per-podServerless, zero-ops$70/month (starter)
WeaviateFree (OSS) or $0.10/hr cloudHybrid search (vector + keyword)$0 (self-hosted) to $72 (cloud)
MilvusFree (OSS) or $0.70/hr (Zilliz Cloud)Billion-scale vectors$0 (self-hosted)

anihpj recommendation: pgvector on existing RDS PostgreSQL — zero additional cost. 50M job embeddings at 768 dimensions = ~150GB. Query latency: <5ms with IVFFlat index. Cost: $0 extra (already paying for RDS). vs Pinecone: $500+/month for same scale.

📝 Practice Questions — Chapter 15

Q1

Why are LLM inference costs fundamentally different from traditional ML model inference?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

LLMs have per-token pricing, quadratic attention costs, and massive memory requirements. Context length drives cost: 32K context = 1,024× more compute than 1K. A single GPT-4 call with 128K context can cost $10+.

Q2

What is PagedAttention (vLLM) and why is it critical for LLM serving costs?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Manages KV-cache in fixed-size pages instead of contiguous memory, eliminating fragmentation and waste. Result: 2-4× higher throughput, 50%+ less memory. Use vLLM for any LLM serving.

Q3

What's the model cascade strategy for LLM API cost optimization?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

Route 80% of requests to cheap models (GPT-4o-mini, Llama 8B), escalate only 20% to expensive models (GPT-4o). Blended cost: 75% cheaper than routing everything to the most capable model.

Q4

At what daily token volume does self-hosting Llama 3 8B become cheaper than API?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

~70M tokens/day. Below this, API (Together AI at $0.20/M tokens) is cheaper. Above this, the fixed GPU cost ($4,139/month for 1×A10G) beats per-token API pricing.

Q5

How does RAG reduce LLM costs compared to context stuffing?

Click to reveal answer
✅ ANSWER

RAG retrieves only relevant documents instead of stuffing everything into the prompt. Embedding + retrieval is 100-1000× cheaper than processing long contexts through the LLM. anihpj: RAG saves 80% on LLM costs for job matching.

Chapter 16: MLOps Pipeline Costs🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 16: MLOps Pipeline Costs — The Glue That Holds AI Together (And Costs Money)

MLOps is the "everything else" of AI — data pipelines, CI/CD, model registries, experiment tracking, feature stores. While GPUs get the spotlight, MLOps infrastructure quietly adds 20-40% to your total AI bill. This chapter maps every MLOps cost center and shows you how to optimize each one with anihpj examples.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours🔄 MLOps🎯 Core
🎓 Certification RelevanceLevel 3: Licensing & SaaS Decisions

16.1 The Full MLOps Cost Picture — Beyond Training and Inference

MLOps Pipeline Cost Breakdown — anihpj
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    MLOps PIPELINE COST — anihpj Monthly View                     │
  │  STAGE              │ TOOL              │ COST/MO  │ %    │ OPTIMIZATION       │
  │  ───────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────┼──────┼────────────────────│
  │  Data Ingestion     │ Airflow + RAPIDS  │ $1,200   │  8%  │ Spot, GPU ETL      │
  │  Data Validation    │ Great Expectations │ $200    │  1%  │ Run with training   │
  │  Model Training     │ PyTorch + H100    │ $15,000  │ 60%  │ Spot, FP16, FSDP   │
  │  Model Evaluation   │ MLflow            │ $300     │  2%  │ Share eval GPU      │
  │  Model Registry     │ MLflow/S3         │ $200     │  1%  │ Lifecycle policies  │

  │                     │                   │ ─────────│      │                    │
  │  TOTAL              │                   │ $23,700  │ 100% │                    │
  │  🔑 Training (60%) + Deployment (26%) = 86%. Focus optimization there.        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The hidden costs: CI/CD runners with GPU tests ($200/month), experiment tracking storage growth ($100/month accumulating), data duplication across teams ($300/month in redundant copies), idle dev environments ($500/month). These "small" costs add up to $1,000+/month — easy to miss, easy to fix.

16.2 CI/CD for ML — Cost-Aware Pipelines

🔧
GPU Testing in CI/CD

Don't test on GPU every commit. Strategy: CPU tests on every PR (fast, free), GPU integration tests on merge to main only. Cost: GPU CI runner: $0.53/hr (T4 spot). If GPU tests run on every commit (50/day × 10min = 8.3 hrs/day = $4.40/day = $132/month). With merge-only strategy (5/day): $13/month — 90% savings. anihpj: GitHub Actions + self-hosted T4 runner.

💰
Infracost in CI/CD

Add infracost diff to every PR. Shows estimated monthly cost change BEFORE merge. "This PR: +$42/month (adds 1×A10G for staging)". Engineers see cost impact at code review time — the most effective moment for cost awareness. Cost: Free (OSS). Setup: 1 hour.

Staged deployments: Dev (CPU/T4 spot, $0.53/hr) → Staging (1×A10G, $5.67/hr) → Production (2×A10G, $11.34/hr). Each stage costs progressively more — don't run staging on production-sized hardware.

GitHub Actions vs Self-Hosted GPU Runners

Runner TypeCostGPUBest For
GitHub Actions (Managed)$0.008/min (Linux)❌ No GPUCPU tests only
Self-Hosted T4 (Spot)$0.009/min ($0.53/hr)✅ 16GBGPU CI tests
Self-Hosted A10G (Reserved)$0.095/min ($5.67/hr)✅ 24GBFull model validation

anihpj strategy: GitHub Actions runners for all CPU tests (lint, unit, integration). Self-hosted T4 spot runner on EKS for GPU tests on merge to main. Result: GPU CI costs reduced from $132/month (every-commit GPU tests) to $13/month (merge-only).

16.3 Model Registry & Storage Costs — Keep What You Need

📦
Registry Cleanup Strategy

Keep: Production models (active), staging candidates (last 3), best experiment artifacts (top 3 per project). Archive: Models >90 days old → S3 Glacier (83% cheaper). Delete: Failed experiments, intermediate checkpoints, dev models >30 days. anihpj: 50 model versions × 440MB = 22GB → after cleanup: 5 versions × 440MB = 2.2GB. Savings: $0.46 → $0.05/month (tiny but compounds with scale).

📊
MLflow Storage Growth

MLflow stores artifacts, metrics, and parameters for every run. Growth rate: ~10MB/run × 100 runs/week = 1GB/week = 52GB/year. Mitigation: (1) Set artifact retention: delete after 90 days. (2) Use S3 lifecycle policies on MLflow bucket. (3) Move old experiments to cold storage. Cost without management: $1.20/month (S3 Standard). Not about the money — about not having 500 failed experiments cluttering your registry.

16.4 Experiment Tracking Platform Costs — MLflow vs W&B vs Neptune

PlatformPricingAnnual Cost (5 users)Best For
MLflow (Self-Hosted)Free OSS + infra costs$600 (EC2 + S3)Cost-sensitive, K8s expertise
W&B (Managed)$50/user/month (Teams)$3,000Zero-ops, best UX, collaboration
Neptune.ai$49/user/month$2,940Enterprise, metadata-heavy

anihpj recommendation: Self-hosted MLflow. 5× cheaper than W&B at small scale. 10× cheaper at 20+ users. Trade-off: requires K8s management (but anihpj already runs EKS). Break-even: If your team spends >2 hours/month maintaining MLflow, managed W&B is worth it.

16.5 Feature Store & Data Pipeline Costs — Compute Once, Serve Many

Feature stores eliminate redundant computation — the biggest hidden MLOps cost. anihpj: Before feature store, 3 teams recomputed job embeddings independently ($150/month). After Feast feature store: compute once ($50), serve to all teams. Savings: $100/month. Batch vs Online: Batch feature computation on spot instances (cheap, hourly/daily). Online feature serving for real-time inference (expensive, low-latency). Use batch for 80%+ of features. Online only for user-specific or real-time features.

Chapter 17: Container & Kubernetes Costs for AI🟡 Intermediate

Chapter 17: Container & Kubernetes Costs for AI — Docker, K8s, and GPU Economics

Docker and Kubernetes are the backbone of modern AI deployment. But containers and orchestration add their own cost dimensions — image storage, GPU scheduling, namespace chargeback, and multi-tenancy. This chapter covers K8s-specific FinOps for AI with real anihpj EKS configurations.

🟡 Intermediate⏱️ ~1.5 hours☸️ Kubernetes🐳 Docker
🎓 Certification RelevanceLevel 3: Architecting & Workload Placement

17.1 Docker for AI Workloads — Cost-Optimized Images

🐳
Image Size Economics

Large images cost in 3 ways: (1) ECR storage ($0.10/GB/month), (2) Pull time (wasted GPU seconds), (3) CI/CD build time. Multi-stage builds: anihpj Docker image reduced from 5.2GB → 2.8GB (46% smaller). Savings: $0.48/month storage + faster pulls (GPU idle during image pull costs $5.67/hr!). Base image: Use nvidia/cuda:12.1-runtime (not devel) for production. Saves 2GB.

🗜️
Dockerfile Best Practices

(1) Multi-stage builds — separate build from runtime. (2) .dockerignore — exclude checkpoints, datasets, venvs. (3) Layer ordering — put rarely-changing layers first (CUDA, PyTorch) for cache hits. (4) Slim base: python:3.11-slim instead of python:3.11. (5) Combine RUN commands to reduce layers. Result: 3-5× faster CI/CD builds, cheaper ECR storage.

anihpj Cost-Optimized Dockerfile

Multi-Stage Dockerfile — 5.2GB → 2.8GB (46% Smaller)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Stage 1: Build dependencies (DISCARDED after build)                         │
  │  FROM nvidia/cuda:12.1-runtime-ubuntu22.04 AS base                           │
  │  FROM base AS builder                                                        │
  │  RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y python3.11 python3-pip             │
  │  COPY requirements.txt .                                                     │
  │  RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt                          │
  │                                                                              │
  │  # Stage 2: Production image (SLIM — no build tools!)                        │
  │  FROM base                                                                   │
  │  COPY --from=builder /usr/local/lib/python3.11 /usr/local/lib/              │
  │  COPY anihpj/ /app/                                                          │
  │  WORKDIR /app                                                                │
  │  EXPOSE 8000                                                                │
  │  CMD ["python", "manage.py", "runserver", "0.0.0.0:8000"]                 │
  │                                                                              │
  │  📊 RESULT: 5.2GB → 2.8GB │ 💰 Saves: ~$200/month (ECR + pull time + CI/CD) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

17.2 Kubernetes GPU Scheduling & Cost — Bin Packing and Node Affinity

📦
GPU Bin Packing

Problem: Each GPU node costs $5.67-98/hr. A pod using 1 GPU on an 8-GPU node wastes 7 GPUs if not packed. Solution: Use nvidia.com/gpu resource requests/limits. Schedule multiple pods per node. anihpj: 3 inference pods × 1 GPU each on 1 g5.12xlarge (4 GPU) = 75% node GPU utilization. 4th GPU for dev. Bin packing is the #1 K8s GPU cost optimization.

🎯
Node Affinity & Taints

Node affinity: Schedule training pods on H100 nodes, inference on A10G, dev on T4. Taints/Tolerations: Dedicate expensive H100 nodes to training only — prevent dev pods from accidentally landing there. Cost impact: Prevents $98/hr GPUs from running $0.53/hr workloads. anihpj: nodeSelector: gpu-type: a10g on inference deployments.

GPU Descheduling — Evict Idle Workloads

Problem: A dev pod using 1 GPU at 5% utilization blocks that GPU from being used by training jobs. Solution: Use K8s Descheduler to evict pods from underutilized GPU nodes. Policy: "If GPU utilization <10% for >1hr AND pod priority < high → evict." Result: Frees up stranded GPU resources for higher-priority workloads. Alternatives: (1) KEDA scaled objects with idle timeout, (2) Custom CronJob that checks GPU util and scales deployments to zero. anihpj: Descheduler runs every 30 min. Auto-evicts idle dev GPU pods at night — reclaims 2-4 GPUs nightly.

Kubernetes GPU Pod YAML — Cost-Optimized Deployment

anihpj Inference Deployment — GPU Pod with Cost Tags
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: apps/v1                                                         │
  │  kind: Deployment                                                            │
  │  metadata:                                                                   │
  │    name: anihpj-inference                                                    │
  │    labels:                                                                   │
  │      cost-center: ai-ml-engineering    # ← FinOps tag                         │
  │      workload-type: inference          # ← Workload classification           │
  │      model-name: jobbert-v3            # ← Model identity                     │
  │  spec:                                                                       │
  │    replicas: 3                          # Autoscaled by KEDA                 │
  │    template:                                                                 │
  │      metadata:                                                               │
  │        labels: { app: anihpj-inference, cost-center: ai-ml-engineering }     │
  │      spec:                                                                   │
  │        nodeSelector:                                                          │
  │          gpu-type: a10g                  # Schedule only on A10G nodes       │
  │        tolerations:                                                           │
  │        - key: "workload"                                                      │
  │          operator: "Equal"                                                    │
  │          value: "inference"                                                   │
  │          effect: "NoSchedule"             # Tolerate inference node taint    │
  │        containers:                                                           │
  │        - name: triton-server                                                 │
  │          image: nvcr.io/nvidia/tritonserver:24.01-py3                        │
  │          resources:                                                          │
  │            limits:                                                           │
  │              nvidia.com/gpu: 1            # 1 GPU per pod                    │
  │            requests:                                                         │
  │              nvidia.com/gpu: 1                                                │
  │              memory: "16Gi"                                                   │
  │              cpu: "4"                                                        │
  │                                                                              │
  │  💰 COST: 3 pods × 1 A10G × $5.67/hr = $17.01/hr (peak)                     │
  │  🏷️ Kubecost automatically allocates $17.01/hr to cost-center=ai-ml-eng     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

EKS vs GKE vs AKS — Managed K8s Cost Comparison

ServiceControl Plane CostGPU Node TypesBest For
AWS EKS$0.10/hr ($73/month)g5, p4d, p5NVIDIA GPU flexibility, AWS ecosystem
GCP GKE Autopilot$0 (included)a2, g2Zero-ops GPU K8s. Pay per pod, not per node.

EKS Fargate vs Managed Node Groups: Fargate (serverless pods, $0.04/vCPU-hr) — good for CPU workloads, NO GPU support. Managed Node Groups — required for GPU. GKE Autopilot: Only K8s service with GPU support in serverless mode — pod-level billing, no node management. anihpj: EKS Managed Node Groups for GPU workloads (Autopilot/Fargate don't support GPUs on AWS).

17.4 GPU Cluster Multi-Tenancy & Chargeback

🧩
MIG (Multi-Instance GPU)

Partitions 1 A100/H100 into up to 7 isolated GPU instances. Use case: 3 teams share 1 A100. MIG creates 3 partitions (2g.20gb each). Each team gets guaranteed GPU resources — no noisy neighbor. Cost sharing: Team A pays for their MIG slice based on GPU fraction. vs Time-slicing: MIG = hardware isolation + guaranteed memory. Time-slicing = software sharing + memory contention.

⚙️
MPS (Multi-Process Service)

MPS (NVIDIA): Allows multiple CUDA processes to share one GPU context. Lower overhead than time-slicing, better for cooperative sharing. vs MIG: MPS = software-level sharing within one GPU (processes can interfere). MIG = hardware-level partitioning (no interference). Use MPS when: Single team sharing GPU across their own processes. Use MIG when: Multiple teams need guaranteed, isolated GPU resources for chargeback.

💰
K8s Namespace Chargeback

Kubecost allocates GPU costs per namespace: ml-training: $15,000/month, ml-inference: $6,500/month, ml-dev: $2,000/month. Chargeback implementation: (1) Tag namespaces with team/cost-center labels. (2) Kubecost aggregates costs by label. (3) Monthly report shows per-team GPU spend. (4) Finance deducts from team budgets. anihpj: Full namespace-level chargeback since Month 6.

17.5 anihpj Kubernetes GPU Cost Breakdown — Real Numbers

anihpj EKS Cluster — GPU Cost Per Namespace
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    anihpj EKS GPU COST — Monthly View                            │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ NAMESPACE          │ GPUs            │ COST/MO   │ TEAM    │ SPOT?      ││
  │  │ ───────────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────┼─────────┼────────────││
  │  │ ml-training        │ 8×H100 (spot)   │ $3,840    │ AI Res  │ ✅ 88% off││
  │  │ ml-inference       │ 2×A10G (reserved)│ $6,500   │ Platform│ ❌ steady  ││
  │  │ ml-dev             │ 1×A10G + 2×T4   │ $2,000    │ All     │ ✅ mixed   ││
  │  │ ml-data-processing │ 2×A10G (spot)    │ $960     │ Data    │ ✅ batch   ││
  │  │ monitoring         │ (shared CPU)     │ $300      │ DevOps  │ N/A       ││
  │  │ ───────────────────┼─────────────────┼───────────┼─────────┼────────────││
  │  │ TOTAL              │ 13 GPUs          │ $13,600   │         │ 71% savings││
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│
  │  💰 Without spot/optimization: $47,000/month. With: $13,600/month (71% off!)│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📊 Chapter 17: What We Learned

🐳
DockerMulti-stage builds: 46% smaller images, faster pulls
📦
Bin PackingPack pods onto GPU nodes. 75%+ node GPU utilization.
📊
KubecostPer-namespace GPU cost. Free OSS. 2-hour setup.
🧩
MIGPartition 1 GPU into 7. Hardware isolation for multi-tenant.
💰
