Chapter 29: Practice Questions — Full Exam Simulation🎯 Exam

Chapter 29: 60 Practice Questions Across All 5 Domains

This chapter simulates the real exam with 60 questions distributed exactly as the real exam: Domain 1 (12 Qs), Domain 2 (15 Qs), Domain 3 (15 Qs), Domain 4 (10 Qs), Domain 5 (8 Qs). Each question has answer AND explanation. Treat this as your final exam readiness check.

🎯 Exam⏱️ ~2 hours📝 60 Qs

29.1 Domain 1: FinOps Fundamentals (12 Questions)

Q1

What are the 6 FinOps Principles?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

(1) Teams collaborate, (2) Decisions are driven by business value, (3) Everyone takes ownership, (4) FinOps data is accessible and timely, (5) A centralized team drives FinOps, (6) Take advantage of variable cloud cost model.

EXPLANATION

These 6 principles underpin ALL FinOps decisions. In exam questions, the correct answer often maps back to one of these principles. Example: "Should finance control all cloud spending?" → NO, because Principle 1 says teams collaborate and Principle 3 says everyone takes ownership.

Q2

What are the 3 phases of the FinOps lifecycle?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Inform → Optimize → Operate. Inform (visibility, allocation, benchmarking), Optimize (right-sizing, rate optimization, workload management), Operate (continuous improvement, automation, governance).

EXPLANATION

The phases are sequential but iterative. You don't "finish" Inform and move to Optimize — you continuously cycle through all three. Exam tip: If a question asks "What to do FIRST," the answer is almost always in the Inform phase (visibility before optimization).

Q3

Name the 5 FinOps Personas and their primary concerns.

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Executive: Strategy, ROI. Finance: Budgeting, forecasting. Engineering: Cost-efficient architecture. Practitioner: Day-to-day FinOps operations. Product: Feature cost vs value.

EXPLANATION

Each persona needs different reports and speaks a different language. Engineering needs per-pod cost; Finance needs P&L alignment; Executives need unit economics. The Practitioner is the glue that connects all personas.

Q4

What are the Crawl, Walk, Run maturity stages?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Crawl: Basic visibility, spreadsheets, manual processes. Walk: Dashboards, anomaly alerts, spot usage, weekly reviews. Run: Automation, chargeback, CI/CD integration, continuous optimization.

EXPLANATION

Maturity is assessed per domain, not overall. You can be Run in Cost Optimization but Crawl in Governance. The goal isn't "Run in everything" — it's matching maturity to organizational needs. A 5-person startup at Walk may be perfectly appropriate.

Q5

What's the difference between showback and chargeback?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Showback: "Here's what you spent" — informational only. Chargeback: "This is deducted from your budget" — financial accountability.

EXPLANATION

Showback builds awareness; chargeback creates accountability. Use showback at Crawl/Walk, chargeback at Run. Jumping to chargeback too early creates fear — engineers hide costs instead of optimizing.

Q6

What is the difference between Capex and Opex in cloud FinOps?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Capex (Capital Expenditure): Upfront investment in owned hardware (buying GPUs). Opex (Operational Expenditure): Ongoing pay-as-you-go (cloud GPU rental). Cloud shifts AI from Capex to Opex, enabling experimentation without large upfront commitment.

EXPLANATION

This is foundational to FinOps: cloud converts fixed costs to variable costs. A $100K GPU purchase (Capex) becomes $1.47/hr rental (Opex). This flexibility is exactly what FinOps Principle 6 ("Take advantage of variable cloud cost model") is about.

Q7

What is the purpose of cost allocation tags and name the 3 most important ones?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Tags enable cost attribution to teams/projects/environments. Top 3: Environment (prod/dev/staging), Team/CostCenter (who owns this), Component (which service). Without tags, you can't do showback or chargeback.

EXPLANATION

Tagging is THE prerequisite for FinOps maturity. AWS allows cost allocation tags to appear in CUR reports within 24 hours. Untagged resources = untrackable costs. anihpj uses 6 mandatory tags enforced by Cloud Custodian. Crawl stage: <50% tagged. Walk: 80%+. Run: 100% auto-tagged.

Q8

Explain the difference between unblended, blended, and amortized costs in AWS.

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Unblended: Raw cost per resource (shows discounts). Blended: Averaged across accounts in an organization (hides discounts). Amortized: Spreads upfront RI/SP payments across the commitment period (best for true cost tracking).

EXPLANATION

For FinOps, use AMORTIZED costs — they show the true economic cost including reservation fees spread over time. Unblended costs spike on RI purchase day (misleading). Amortized costs smooth this and show real monthly spend. This matters for accurate showback and unit economics.

📋 Domain 1: 8/12 Questions Shown

Additional Domain 1 topics: Cloud billing fundamentals (Ch 3), Tagging strategy (Ch 4), Cost allocation methods (Ch 4), FinOps team structure (Ch 26). Review those chapters for the full Domain 1 preparation.

29.2 Domain 2: AI/ML Cost Understanding (15 Questions)

Q6

What are the 4 key GPU cost drivers in AI workloads?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

(1) GPU type (A10G $1.47/hr vs H100 $98/hr), (2) GPU count (single vs multi-GPU vs distributed), (3) Duration (training hours × inference 24/7), (4) Utilization (idle GPU = wasted money).

EXPLANATION

GPU cost = Type × Count × Hours × Utilization rate. A 32% utilized H100 costs the same as a 100% utilized H100 — you're paying for the GPU whether you use it or not. This is why GPU utilization is the #1 optimization metric.

Q7

How do you allocate shared GPU cluster costs across teams?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Use Kubecost by namespace + mandatory Team/CostCenter tags. Each team gets their own namespace. Kubecost maps cloud billing to namespace-level GPU usage. Tags enable cross-namespace cost attribution.

EXPLANATION

Without namespace separation and tagging, shared GPU costs are a black box. The FinOps approach: (1) Tag everything, (2) Namespace per team, (3) Kubecost for K8s-native cost mapping, (4) Showback first, (5) Chargeback when mature. Never split costs evenly (unfair to efficient teams).

Q8

What are the 3 cloud pricing models for GPUs and their typical savings?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

On-Demand: Full price, no commitment. Reserved/Savings Plan: 30-72% savings, 1-3yr commitment. Spot/Preemptible: 60-90% savings, can be terminated with 2 min notice.

EXPLANATION

For AI: Training → Spot (with checkpointing). Inference → Reserved/SP (need reliability). Dev/Test → Spot. The optimal mix is typically 30% reserved + 70% spot. Use the right model for the right workload — never 100% on-demand at scale.

Q9

What cloud storage tiers should you use for AI training data vs active model checkpoints?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Training data: S3 Standard/Intelligent Tiering (frequent access). Active checkpoints: S3 Standard (last 3). Old checkpoints: S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval (80% cheaper, milliseconds access). Archived models: S3 Glacier Deep Archive (95% cheaper).

EXPLANATION

AI generates massive storage. A typical training run produces 50+ checkpoints at 2GB each = 100GB. Keeping all in Standard costs $2.30/month. Moving old ones to Glacier: $0.10/month. The FinOps principle: match storage tier to access frequency. anihpj auto-archives checkpoints >30 days old.

Q10

What GPU metrics should you monitor for cost management and what tool captures them?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Key metrics: GPU Utilization (%), GPU Memory Used (GB), Power Draw (Watts), Temperature (°C). Tools: DCGM Exporter (NVIDIA) → Prometheus → Grafana. For cost correlation: Kubecost adds $/pod overlay.

EXPLANATION

GPU utilization < 40% = you're burning money. DCGM (Data Center GPU Manager) exports these metrics natively. Combine with Kubecost to see: "This pod is using 32% of an A10G GPU at $1.47/hr — that's $1.00/hr wasted." Without these metrics, you're flying blind on GPU cost efficiency.

💡 Domain 2: 6/15 Questions Shown

Also study: GPU hardware (Ch 5), Data costs (Ch 6), Monitoring & Observability (Ch 7), Cloud billing (Ch 3), Tagging (Ch 4).

29.3 Domain 3: AI Cost Optimization (15 Questions)

Q9

What are the top 4 techniques for reducing training costs?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

(1) Spot instances (60-90% savings) with checkpointing. (2) Mixed precision (FP16/BF16, 2× speedup). (3) Optimal batch size (max GPU memory utilization). (4) Early stopping (stop when validation loss plateaus).

EXPLANATION

These are ranked by ROI: Spot gives the biggest $ savings with minimal engineering effort. Mixed precision is almost free (AMP library, 1 line of code). Batch size tuning requires experimentation. Early stopping saves compute for marginal accuracy gains.

Q10

What is quantization and how much does it reduce inference costs?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Quantization reduces model precision from FP32→FP16→INT8→INT4. FP16: 2× smaller, 2× faster, <1% accuracy loss. INT8: 4× smaller, 3-4× faster, 1-3% accuracy loss. Inference cost drops proportionally.

EXPLANATION

A FP32 model on A10G costs $1.47/hr. Quantize to INT8 → now fits on T4 ($0.52/hr) with same throughput. That's 65% cost reduction. Quantization is the highest-ROI single optimization for inference. Tools: TensorRT, ONNX Runtime, PyTorch quantization APIs.

Q11

LoRA vs Full Fine-Tuning: When do you use which?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

LoRA: Trains small adapter weights ($25, 6h). Best for task adaptation, cost-sensitive projects. Full FT: Updates all weights ($650, 24h). Best for domain adaptation requiring fundamental behavior change.

EXPLANATION

LoRA trains 0.1-1% of parameters, giving 90% of full FT quality at 5% of the cost. Use LoRA by default unless you have evidence that full FT is needed. anihpj uses LoRA for job description generation — $25 one-time cost vs $650 for full FT.

Q12

What is model distillation and how does it reduce inference costs?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Model distillation: Train a smaller "student" model to mimic a larger "teacher" model. Student is 2-10× smaller, 2-5× faster, with 90-97% of teacher accuracy. Runs on cheaper GPUs, has lower latency, and lower cost per inference.

EXPLANATION

Example: BERT-base (110M params) → DistilBERT (66M params). 40% smaller, 60% faster, 95% accuracy. If BERT inference costs $0.01/request on A10G, DistilBERT costs $0.004 on T4 — 60% savings at scale. The teacher training cost is one-time; the student savings are perpetual.

Q13

Your training job uses 4 GPUs but GPU utilization is only 45%. What should you investigate first?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Check the data pipeline bottleneck. Low GPU utilization usually means GPUs are waiting for data. Solutions: (1) Increase batch size to fill GPU memory, (2) Add more CPU workers for data loading, (3) Use in-memory caching (e.g., tf.data.cache()), (4) Pre-process data offline and store as TFRecords.

EXPLANATION

GPUs are fast — they chew through data faster than CPUs can prepare it. A GPU at 45% utilization is like paying full price for a sports car but only driving it at 45 mph. The fix costs nothing (config changes) but can double throughput, halving training cost. This is the most common and most fixable GPU waste pattern.

Q14

What is batching and how does it improve inference cost efficiency?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Batching: Process multiple inference requests together on the GPU instead of one-at-a-time. Increases throughput 2-5× without needing a bigger GPU. Dynamic batching waits a few ms to accumulate requests into optimal batch sizes.

EXPLANATION

A GPU processing 1 request at a time uses maybe 20% of its compute capacity. Batching 8 requests together uses 90%+ capacity — 4.5× more throughput for the same GPU cost. Tools: NVIDIA Triton Inference Server has built-in dynamic batching. Trade-off: slightly higher latency per request (few ms) for dramatically lower cost per request.

💡 Domain 3: 8/15 Questions Shown

Also study: Training optimization (Ch 8-10), Inference optimization (Ch 11-15), Spot/Reserved strategies (Ch 22), Model optimization techniques.

29.4 Domain 4: FinOps Operations & Governance (10 Questions)

Q12

What tool provides per-pod GPU cost visibility in Kubernetes?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Kubecost. Open-source (free tier). Installs via Helm in 5 minutes. Maps cloud billing to Kubernetes objects. Shows GPU cost per namespace, deployment, pod, and label.

EXPLANATION

Kubecost is the standard for K8s-native FinOps. It directly integrates with AWS CUR/Azure EA/GCP BigQuery. Enterprise tier ($499/month) adds SSO, multi-cluster, and chargeback. For single-cluster AI workloads, the free tier is sufficient.

Q13

What are the 3 most important governance policies for AI workloads?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

(1) Tagging policy: All resources MUST have cost-center/team tags (auto-stop untagged). (2) GPU usage policy: GPU type allowlist + max GPUs per user. (3) Budget policy: Team budgets with progressive alerts (80%→90%→100% hard stop).

EXPLANATION

Policies without enforcement are just suggestions. Use Cloud Custodian (free OSS) for policy-as-code enforcement. Example: A GPU without tags is auto-stopped after 1 hour. This ensures 100% compliance without manual policing.

Q14

What is the difference between RI and Savings Plan for GPU workloads?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

RI (Reserved Instance): Commit to specific GPU type + region. 40-72% savings. Less flexible. SP (Savings Plan): Commit to $/hr spend. Any GPU, any region. 30-50% savings. More flexible. For AI GPUs, SP is usually better (GPU availability varies).

EXPLANATION

Exam tip: If the question mentions "predictable, steady GPU workload with known instance type" → RI. If it mentions "variable GPU usage" or "may change instance types" → SP. The key tradeoff is savings rate vs flexibility. Don't confuse RI (instance-specific) with SP (dollar-based).

Q15

What is the role of the FinOps Practitioner in an AI organization?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

The FinOps Practitioner is the day-to-day operator who monitors costs, sends anomaly alerts, creates reports, and drives optimization initiatives. They bridge Engineering (cost data) and Finance (budgets). In AI orgs, they specialize in GPU economics and ML experiment cost tracking.

EXPLANATION

The Practitioner doesn't optimize alone — they enable teams to optimize themselves by providing visibility and tooling. Key skills: cloud billing APIs, K8s/Kubecost, SQL for CUR analysis, and communication (translating $/GPU-hr to business metrics). This is the fastest-growing FinOps role in AI companies.

💡 Domain 4: 6/10 Questions Shown

Also study: K8s cost (Ch 17), Rate optimization (Ch 22), Anomaly detection (Ch 23), Green FinOps (Ch 25), Building practice (Ch 26).

29.5 Domain 5: Unit Economics & Business Value (8 Questions)

Q19

How do you calculate unit cost for an AI inference service?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

Unit Cost = Total Monthly AI Cost / Business Metric. For anihpj: $14,000/month / 300,000 inferences = $0.047 per inference. Choose a metric your business understands.

EXPLANATION

Unit economics translate cloud bills into business language. The CTO cares about "$0.047 per job recommendation" not "$14K in GPU spend." This enables ROI conversations with stakeholders who don't understand cloud billing.

Q20

What is the formula for AI ROI and how does anihpj calculate it?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

ROI = (Revenue from AI - AI Cost) / AI Cost. anihpj: ($50,000 - $14,000) / $14,000 = 257% ROI.

EXPLANATION

AI without measured ROI is a cost center; AI with measured ROI is a profit center. Track ROI quarterly. If it drops below 100%, investigate cost increases, value declines, or measurement issues.

Q21

How should you forecast AI costs for the next quarter using bottom-up method?

▶ Show Answer & Explanation
ANSWER

(1) Project inference volume (requests × growth rate), (2) × cost per inference, (3) + training costs (experiments × GPU-hrs each), (4) + data storage growth, (5) + 15% buffer for experimentation. Forecast inference and training separately.

EXPLANATION

AI costs have two components: variable (inference scales with users) and semi-fixed (training scales with team activity). anihpj: Inference = $0.047/req × 350K = $16,450. Training = 4 experiments × $35 = $140. Total + 15% buffer ≈ $19K.

💡 Domain 5: 5/8 Questions Shown

Also study: Unit economics (Ch 18), Budgeting & forecasting (Ch 19), Multi-cloud (Ch 20), ROI analysis, stakeholder communication.

30.2 AI-Specific Quick Reference

🎮
GPU Pricing Quick Ref

T4: $0.52/hr (16 GB). A10G: $1.47/hr (24 GB). A100: $3.06/hr (40 GB) / $4.10/hr (80 GB). H100: $98/hr (80 GB). Spot discount: 60-90% off these rates.

⚡
Optimization Speedup Factors

Mixed Precision (FP16): 2× speedup. Quantization (INT8): 3-4× speedup. Batching: 2-5× throughput. Model Distillation: 2-10× smaller model. TensorRT: 2-3× inference speedup.

📐
Must-Know Formulas

Training Cost: GPU-hrs × $/hr × GPU count. Inference Cost: Requests × Cost/request. Unit Cost: Total monthly / Business metric. GPU Util: Actual TFLOPS / Theoretical. Savings Rate: (On-demand - Optimized) / On-demand.

🛠️
Tools Mapped to Use Cases

Cost Visibility: Kubecost, AWS Cost Explorer. Policy Automation: Cloud Custodian. IaC Cost: Infracost. Experiment Tracking: MLflow, W&B. Carbon: CodeCarbon, Kepler. LLM Cost: Langfuse, Helicone.

30.3 Final Practice Quiz (8 Rapid-Fire Questions)

Q1

Spot instances are suitable for: A) Real-time inference B) Training with checkpointing C) Database servers D) All of the above

▶ Show Answer
ANSWER

B — Training with checkpointing. Training can tolerate 2-min interruption if checkpoints save every 15 min. Inference and databases need guaranteed availability.

Q2

Which FinOps phase comes first? A) Optimize B) Operate C) Inform D) Automate

▶ Show Answer
ANSWER

C — Inform. You can't optimize what you can't see. Visibility always comes first. Inform → Optimize → Operate.

Q3

RI gives ___% savings but locks you to ___. SP gives ___% savings with ___ flexibility.

▶ Show Answer
ANSWER

RI: 30-72% savings, locks to specific GPU type/region. SP: 30-50% savings, any GPU/region. RI = more savings, less flexibility. SP = more flexibility, less savings.

Q4

Showback = ___ ; Chargeback = ___

▶ Show Answer
ANSWER

Showback = informational ("you spent $X"). Chargeback = financial ("$X deducted from your budget").

Q5

What tool provides per-pod GPU cost in Kubernetes?

▶ Show Answer
ANSWER

Kubecost. Free OSS. Helm install. Maps AWS/Azure/GCP billing to K8s objects at namespace/deployment/pod level.

Q6

Quantization from FP32 to INT8: ___× smaller, ___× faster, ___% accuracy loss.

▶ Show Answer
ANSWER

4× smaller, 3-4× faster, 1-3% accuracy loss. Highest-ROI single optimization for inference. Use TensorRT or PyTorch quantization.

Q7

Unit Cost = ?

▶ Show Answer
ANSWER

Total Monthly AI Cost / Business Metric. E.g., $14,000 / 300,000 inferences = $0.047/inference. Translates cloud bills into business language.

Q8

What are the 3 FinOps maturity levels?

▶ Show Answer
ANSWER

Crawl (basic, manual) → Walk (dashboards, alerts) → Run (automation, chargeback). Assessed per domain, not overall.

30.4 What's Next After Certification

🚀
Immediate Next Steps

(1) Add certification to LinkedIn + resume. (2) Apply FinOps to your real AI workloads — start with the 90-day kickstart (Ch 26). (3) Join the FinOps Foundation Community (free Slack). (4) Attend FinOps X (annual conference). (5) Share your anihpj cost optimization wins with your team.

📈
Career Pathway

Certified Practitioner → Apply in real org for 1-2 years → FinOps Professional (advanced cert) → FinOps Leader (strategic role). Also: FinOps for SaaS certification if you work on SaaS products. FinOps is a growing field — AI/ML cost management is one of the hottest specializations.

Review the cram sheet (30.1) the night before, get a good night's sleep, and trust your preparation. Good luck on the FinOps Certified: AI Value exam!
